It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.

Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I probably would’ve called it just being a bit dreamy back then. In grade school, my report cards sometimes said things like “daydreams” or “needs reminders to finish work.” And my mother used to sit beside me while I did homework, just to keep me on track, before I was twelve. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult, I don’t think… I just needed nudging. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I still enjoy the usual things when I get to them. Maybe I have quieter days, but I wouldn’t say things have stopped being enjoyable. I don’t think that’s been a big problem for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday things, like appointments, errands, or where you put items?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s usually not big things, but I’ll put something down “somewhere safe,” like a camera battery, a name tag, or an envelope with sign-up sheets, and then I spend half the morning retracing my steps trying to find it. It’s embarrassing, really. I try to be organized, but those little things do get away from me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also cause difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not working now, but with the museum group, some of the planning bits have sort of… quietly been shifted away from me, I think because I wasn’t keeping on top of them as well as I should.\n\nAnd at home it shows up too, in small ways. I’ve also stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks as I used to, which bothers me. I’m still managing, mostly, but yes… it’s not just in one place. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, especially if it isn’t very interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off tasks because they require a lot of sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like to think of it as avoiding exactly. It’s more that if I know I need to sit down and read through a long exhibit description, or plan out what I’m going to say for a talk, I suddenly notice all sorts of little chores around the house that “need” doing first.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just clearing the decks, but really, sitting with all that reading can feel like pulling teeth. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s not anything too dramatic, but I’ve noticed I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, trying to listen, but then I realize I missed a chunk in the middle and I have to ask them to repeat it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like you heard the sounds but couldn’t make out the words?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I couldn’t make out the words, no. It’s more like… someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them and trying to follow, but then I realize I missed the middle part somehow. So I end up asking them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I probably would’ve called it just being a bit dreamy back then. In grade school, my report cards sometimes said things like “daydreams” or “needs reminders to finish work.” And my mother used to sit beside me while I did homework, just to keep me on track, before I was twelve. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult, I don’t think… I just needed nudging. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I still enjoy the usual things when I get to them. Maybe I have quieter days, but I wouldn’t say things have stopped being enjoyable. I don’t think that’s been a big problem for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday things, like appointments, errands, or where you put items?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s usually not big things, but I’ll put something down “somewhere safe,” like a camera battery, a name tag, or an envelope with sign-up sheets, and then I spend half the morning retracing my steps trying to find it. It’s embarrassing, really. I try to be organized, but those little things do get away from me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also cause difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not working now, but with the museum group, some of the planning bits have sort of… quietly been shifted away from me, I think because I wasn’t keeping on top of them as well as I should.\n\nAnd at home it shows up too, in small ways. I’ve also stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks as I used to, which bothers me. I’m still managing, mostly, but yes… it’s not just in one place. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, especially if it isn’t very interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off tasks because they require a lot of sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like to think of it as avoiding exactly. It’s more that if I know I need to sit down and read through a long exhibit description, or plan out what I’m going to say for a talk, I suddenly notice all sorts of little chores around the house that “need” doing first.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just clearing the decks, but really, sitting with all that reading can feel like pulling teeth. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details, even when you know the material?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not that people are pointing out to me.\n\nI might miss a detail here or there, but… I suppose everyone does that sometimes. If you mean things like knowing the material but still overlooking something obvious, I can’t say I’ve noticed that as a big pattern. Is that alright to say?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, especially if it isn’t very interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off tasks because they require a lot of sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like to think of it as avoiding exactly. It’s more that if I know I need to sit down and read through a long exhibit description, or plan out what I’m going to say for a talk, I suddenly notice all sorts of little chores around the house that “need” doing first.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just clearing the decks, but really, sitting with all that reading can feel like pulling teeth. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details, even when you know the material?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not that people are pointing out to me.\n\nI might miss a detail here or there, but… I suppose everyone does that sometimes. If you mean things like knowing the material but still overlooking something obvious, I can’t say I’ve noticed that as a big pattern. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or keeping your materials in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I’m not perfectly organized or anything, but I haven’t really noticed it causing much trouble.\n\nMy papers or things at home might get a bit scattered now and then, but… I usually know roughly where things are, or I can sort them out if I need to. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s not anything too dramatic, but I’ve noticed I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like you heard the sounds but couldn’t make out the words?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I couldn’t make out the words, no. It’s more like… someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them and trying to follow, but then I realize I missed the middle part somehow. So I end up asking them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. So I end up asking them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I probably would’ve called it just being a bit dreamy back then. In grade school, my report cards sometimes said things like “daydreams” or “needs reminders to finish work.” And my mother used to sit beside me while I did homework, just to keep me on track, before I was twelve. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult, I don’t think… I just needed nudging. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I still enjoy the usual things when I get to them. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday things, like appointments, errands, or where you put items?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s more like… someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them and trying to follow, but then I realize I missed the middle part somehow. So I end up asking them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult, I don’t think… I just needed nudging. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I still enjoy the usual things when I get to them. Maybe I have quieter days, but I wouldn’t say things have stopped being enjoyable. I don’t think that’s been a big problem for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday things, like appointments, errands, or where you put items?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s usually not big things, but I’ll put something down “somewhere safe,” like a camera battery, a name tag, or an envelope with sign-up sheets, and then I spend half the morning retracing my steps trying to find it. It’s embarrassing, really. I try to be organized, but those little things do get away from me. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I suppose so… not every time, but often enough that I notice it.

Like with the volunteer group, if someone gives me a few things to handle, I’ll usually start off fine. I might print the maps, for example, and feel like I’m on top of it. But then I’ll forget to call the museum desk, or I’ll leave the folders only half put together until someone gives me a reminder.

I do understand what they’re asking me to do. It’s more that I don’t always follow it all the way through on my own. I’m not sure if that’s exactly what you mean, but that’s the sort of thing that happens.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s not anything too dramatic, but I’ve noticed I’m having trouble keeping up when people are talking to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, trying to listen, but then I realize I missed a chunk in the middle and I have to ask them to repeat it. It’s a little embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like you heard the sounds but couldn’t make out the words?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like I couldn’t make out the words, no. It’s more like… someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them and trying to follow, but then I realize I missed the middle part somehow. So I end up asking them to run it by me again. It’s not all the time, but enough that I noticed it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of missing the middle happen when you’re reading, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can, a little. Like if I’m reading a gallery packet or some notes, I’ll start off fine, and then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the words but my mind’s kind of drifted. Then I have to go back and read the same part again. It’s not like I can’t read it, I just… lose the thread sometimes, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot, even when there isn’t an obvious reason to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not a lot. I mean, I worry about normal things, I guess, like anyone would, but I wouldn’t say I’m sitting around anxious for no reason. At least, I haven’t really noticed that being a big part of it. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… though I probably would’ve called it just being a bit dreamy back then. In grade school, my report cards sometimes said things like “daydreams” or “needs reminders to finish work.” And my mother used to sit beside me while I did homework, just to keep me on track, before I was twelve. It wasn’t like I was trying to be difficult, I don’t think… I just needed nudging. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than you usually do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I still enjoy the usual things when I get to them. Maybe I have quieter days, but I wouldn’t say things have stopped being enjoyable. I don’t think that’s been a big problem for me. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday things, like appointments, errands, or where you put items?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s usually not big things, but I’ll put something down “somewhere safe,” like a camera battery, a name tag, or an envelope with sign-up sheets, and then I spend half the morning retracing my steps trying to find it. It’s embarrassing, really. I try to be organized, but those little things do get away from me. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do these attention problems also cause difficulties in more than one part of your life, such as both at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I’m not working now, but with the museum group, some of the planning bits have sort of… quietly been shifted away from me, I think because I wasn’t keeping on top of them as well as I should.\n\nAnd at home it shows up too, in small ways. I’ve also stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks as I used to, which bothers me. I’m still managing, mostly, but yes… it’s not just in one place. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, especially if it isn’t very interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off tasks because they require a lot of sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like to think of it as avoiding exactly. It’s more that if I know I need to sit down and read through a long exhibit description, or plan out what I’m going to say for a talk, I suddenly notice all sorts of little chores around the house that “need” doing first.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just clearing the decks, but really, sitting with all that reading can feel like pulling teeth. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details, even when you know the material?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not that people are pointing out to me.\n\nI might miss a detail here or there, but… I suppose everyone does that sometimes. If you mean things like knowing the material but still overlooking something obvious, I can’t say I’ve noticed that as a big pattern. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or keeping your materials in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I’m not perfectly organized or anything, but I haven’t really noticed it causing much trouble.\n\nMy papers or things at home might get a bit scattered now and then, but… I usually know roughly where things are, or I can sort them out if I need to. Is that a fair way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task all the way through, especially if it isn’t very interesting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nDoctor: Do you often put off tasks because they require a lot of sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, though I don’t like to think of it as avoiding exactly. It’s more that if I know I need to sit down and read through a long exhibit description, or plan out what I’m going to say for a talk, I suddenly notice all sorts of little chores around the house that “need” doing first.\n\nI’ll tell myself I’m just clearing the decks, but really, sitting with all that reading can feel like pulling teeth. I do get to it eventually, usually, but I can put it off longer than I should. Does that sound like what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes or miss details, even when you know the material?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not that people are pointing out to me.\n\nI might miss a detail here or there, but… I suppose everyone does that sometimes. If you mean things like knowing the material but still overlooking something obvious, I can’t say I’ve noticed that as a big pattern. Is that alright to say?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or keeping your materials in order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say that’s a big problem for me. I mean, I’m not perfectly organized or anything, but I haven’t really noticed it causing much trouble.\n\nMy papers or things at home might get a bit scattered now and then, but… I usually know roughly where things are, or I can sort them out if I need to. So I’m not sure I’d count that one, unless you mean something more specific?\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem not to listen when they’re speaking to you directly, even when there isn’t an obvious distraction?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen sometimes. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m ignoring people, because I’m really not trying to.\n\nAt the museum, someone might be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I’ve missed the middle part of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again. It’s a bit embarrassing, honestly. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have these attention problems caused significant problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, a bit. I don’t want to overstate it, because I still manage day to day. But with the museum group, I’ve noticed they’ve sort of quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me.\n\nNo one’s been unkind about it, but I can tell. And I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. I hate saying that, because I liked being someone they could count on, but… yes, I suppose it has affected things some.\n\nDoctor: Do you often start tasks but have trouble finishing them, even when you understand what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that happens, though I try not to let it. If someone in the volunteer group gives me a few things to take care of, I’ll usually get the first part done — like printing the maps — but then I might forget to call the museum desk, or I’ll leave the folders sort of half-ready until someone reminds me.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t understand what needs doing. I do. I just… don’t always carry it all the way through unless there’s a nudge. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions and finishing tasks, even when you understand what needs to be done?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose so… not every time, but often enough that I notice it.\n\nLike with the volunteer group, if someone gives me a few things to handle, I’ll usually start off fine. I might print the maps, for example, and feel like I’m on top of it. But then I’ll forget to call the museum desk, or I’ll leave the folders only half put together until someone gives me a reminder.\n\nI do understand what they’re asking me to do. It’s more that I don’t always follow it all the way through on my own. I’m not sure if that’s exactly what you mean, but that’s the sort of thing that happens.\n\nDoctor: Do these kinds of attention lapses happen often—on most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… yes, I think so. Not all day long or anything, and I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but it does come up pretty regularly.\n\nWith the volunteer group especially, if I’m given a few things to handle, I’ll do the first part — like getting the maps printed — and then somehow the next piece slips, like calling the museum desk or finishing the folders. Usually it gets caught when someone reminds me. So… yes, I’d say often enough to be most days in some form."
  }
]

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The question is about excessive worry or anxiety, which is not covered by the provided ADHD inattentive symptom profile items.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes… I suppose I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s usually not big things, but I’ll put something down “somewhere safe,” like a camera battery, a name tag, or an envelope with sign-up sheets, and then I spend half the morning retracing my steps trying to find it.   }

Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes… I think that happens. Not always, but enough that I notice it.\n\nLike, I can sit down with a gallery packet or some notes and I start off perfectly fine, thinking I’ll just get through it. Then after a few minutes I realize my eyes are still going over the page, but my mind has gone somewhere else entirely. So I have to go back and read the same bit again.\n\nIt’s a little frustrating, but I do usually get there eventually. Does that count as trouble staying focused?\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S006\",\"name\":\"Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\",\"description\":\"Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is directly asking about putting off tasks because they require sustained mental effort, which matches this symptom profile.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The question is most directly about difficulty organizing tasks or keeping materials in order, and the provided symptom profile does not include an organization-difficulty item. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.
